# WORKLOG — shared time-stamped state log (append-only, CURRENT file)

Standing user order (2026-09-25): everybody appends every state change here. This log is the manager's debugging paper trail.

**Rotation policy (manager ruling 2026-09-25):** when this file exceeds ~10 MB or at each day boundary, the manager rotates it to `logs/worklog/YYYY-MM-DD[-NN].md` and starts this file fresh. The trail is never lost - archives are committed and greppable.

**Concision rule:** WORKLOG lines are LOG ENTRIES (target ≤ ~500 chars). Full reports, tables and evidence live in `reviews/` and the interrupt files - link them here, do not inline them.

**Line format:** `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM TZ | actor | EVENT | detail` — newest at the bottom, append-only.

Combined-format transaction START/A0/3C/5A/repeated START/A1/read 11 22 33 (ACK,ACK,NACK)/STOP against a Verilog slave model written from the wire rules. New obligations vs i2c_xfer: a repeated START after a 2-byte write phase, a 3-byte read BURST where the master DRIVES SDA low on the 9th clock to continue it, and a stretching slave that owns SCL. Firmware polls the pad for every high-phase edge and dmem[0] counts the polls: 0 with no stretch (non-vacuity, first half) and 70 with one (second half). Transcript: acks=00 00 00 00 read=11 22 33 stretch_polls=0 done=a5; stretched case slave held SCL 1 time, max tLOW 15016 ns (900 clocks) with min tHIGH 5950 ns unchanged. RED evidence: the same TB against the old i2c_xfer.hex reports 2 STARTs->1, 0 read bytes served, 0 ACKs, read=00 00 00 (16 FAILs). Two real defects the TB caught and the firmware fixed: (1) the read ACK released SDA in the same cycle it released SCL - the slave read a NACK (burst stopped after 1 byte) and SDA rose under a rising clock (2 grammar violations); (2) the first completion cap was 400 us for a 1.3 ms transaction, which read as a hang. Not yet in run_all.sh/run_firmware_tests.sh (same-list step after act 3). Chaining to act 2 (SPI mode 3).

2026-09-25 15:22 CDT | fw-timing | CHECKPOINT | DS18B20 READ PATH GREEN on real RTL. The "edge-driven level gate needs one more pass" note was a red herring: the edge-driven model was CORRECT and the firmware was wrong in two places, both found by probing the CPU's own accumulator instead of the wire. (1) THE POLARITY: a 1-Wire read slot is LOW = 1 (the slave holds the line down for a one) and the write slot is the other way about, but ph4 sampled with `JZ rb_was_zero` -- it read a ZERO from a line the sensor was pulling DOWN. The firmware's own header said the opposite of its code. Both bytes came back bit-for-bit complemented (2b -> d4, 01 -> fe), which is the same invisible-defect shape as the DHT11's reversed byte: right bit count, plausible ones, no run-time symptom. (2) THE READ BYTE COUNTER: it was initialised with `STM 10, A` -- dmem[10] is the DELAY ROUTINE's middle-counter TARGET, the very slot the write command counter was moved off one commit earlier -- and decremented at dmem[8], which nothing writes. So the counter started at 0, the loop banked the two CORRECT bytes and then, with 255 slots left instead of 1, walked the byte index up until it banked over dmem[5] (its own index) and came back round to overwrite dmem[0..1] with the tail of the bus. That is why the flush saw `ff ff` from a read path that was correct for all sixteen slots, and why the first fix to look at was the model. Method note: the model was debugged by hooking pc=139 (ph4's IN) and pc=145/149 (the two STM 4,A) and reading a/dmem[4] per sample -- the wire was never the suspect once the decoded samples were seen to be exactly the complement of the model's bits. Still to do before the act is claimed: the TB header's check 6 (read slot widths / sample-inside-window margins) is asserted in prose and NOT yet implemented, the model's payload is truncated to 16 bits by a cap, and the act is not in run_all.sh.
2026-09-25 15:52 CDT | fw-timing | TASK-DONE | BLOCK 2 ACT (a) DS18B20 1-WIRE COMPLETE + WIRED INTO THE REGRESSION. The act is now in run_all.sh (as tb_pe_soc_ds18b20) and run_firmware_tests.sh (ds18b20 assembles, 205 words), and the TB grew the checks its header had been CLAIMING but not implementing: the presence pulse is now inside the datasheet's 60-240 us AND the host's pin_oe is checked released on every clock of it, each write slot's low period is inside its own band (1-15 us for a 1, 60-120 us for a 0), the read slot count is exactly 16, every read slot's initiation pulse is inside 1-15 us, and the SAMPLE INSTANT is measured to sit inside the sensor's data window with >= 2 us of margin at BOTH ends. The sensor model got a tRDV response delay (15 us, the datasheet's own maximum, so "sampled too early" is falsifiable) and a bounded tLOW/tHIGH hold. Two limits recorded in the TB header rather than glossed: tLOW is mid-band and NOT worst case, because with tLOW in 15-60 and tHIGH in 1-15 the two windows OVERLAP and no single instant is correct for every legal sensor timing -- claiming the worst case there would be a stronger-sounding and false claim. And the model's two release edges now come off the PADS rather than off the state machine's position, which is what stopped it losing the master's next release while still inside the previous bit's hold (that bug silently ended the run at 8 slots). Measured: reset 485.7 us, presence 120.0 us, write 1-low 5.0 us / 0-low 64.8 us, commands cc/be, 16 read slots, bytes 2b 01, initiation pulses 6.2-6.3 us, sample 10.8 us after the response and 19.2 us before the hold ends. ~2.2 ms of 60 MHz, 3.2 s of wall. MUTATION GATE: mutate_timing_tb.sh goes 14 -> 26 cases, ALL 26 detected, 0 survived, 0 harness errors, firmware tree cmp-verified byte-identical after the run. The twelve new cases are chosen so the check that fires is the SPECIFIC property claimed, and five of them perturb a COUNTED DELAY CONSTANT rather than the source -- which needed a new peasm flag: the 1-Wire firmware names its delays as symbols (OW_RST, OW_T40) because the value is a fitted instruction count living in peasm's CONSTS table, so a sed of the .pe cannot reach it and a harness that only knew how to sed a .pe would have silently covered no counted delay at all. peasm gained `--const NAME=VALUE`, rejected if NAME is not already in the table so a typo is an error rather than a new symbol that silently assembles. ow-sample-late is the one worth noting: OW_T40 23 -> 45 decodes CORRECTLY (both bytes still right) and is caught by the sample-inside-the-window margin and by nothing else, and ow-write1-width likewise keeps both commands decoding correctly and is caught only by the band check. Both are the reason those two checks exist rather than a byte comparison. FULL SUITE: run_all.sh --fast -j8 -> RTL 41/41, FIRMWARE 34/34, lint clean, all 14 mutation suites OK (timing-TB 26/26).

2026-09-25 17:05 CDT | fw-timing | TASK-DONE | BLOCK 2 ACT (b) NEC INFRARED REMOTE COMPLETE + WIRED IN. firmware/nec_ir.pe (165 words) + tb/tb_pe_soc_ir_nec.v + 7 peasm CONSTS (IR_DATA, IR_H1, IR_H2, IR_LEADR, IR_LEAD, IR_BIT, IR_GAP0, IR_GAP1, IR_LEADGAP). It is the sharpest timing claim in the repository and the only act with NO WIRE: the only thing that leaves the pin is light, so a receiver must find a 38 kHz burst and time the silences between bursts to know what was sent, and nothing resynchronises to anything. MEASURED off the pin: carrier 38,049 Hz (+0.128 % on 38 kHz), LOW half 787.95-794.95 clocks, HIGH half 788.95 (spread 0.01), leader 8988.0 us in 343 carrier cycles, leader gap 4504.0 us, eight data bursts of 552.0 us, gaps 4 long (zeros) and 4 short (ones), stop burst 552.0 us in 22 cycles, decoded a5, 32 ms of 60 MHz. The two half periods are SEPARATE fitted constants on different (n2,n3) pairs ((2,44) and (2,34)) because the phase ladder costs 7 clocks more coming out of phase 1 than out of phase 0, and the fit lands both halves on 789 clocks -- the same "equalised branches" discipline the WS2812 act found twice. FIVE defects, all of the same invisible shape, all recorded in the sources: (1) THE EIGHT-BIT IMMEDIATE, TWICE. 9 ms is 342 carrier cycles and `LDI A, 342` assembled to `LDI A, 86`; the frame that left the pin was a perfectly good 38 kHz carrier carrying a 2.3 ms "9 ms" leader. The leader is now IR_LEADR runs of IR_LEAD cycles. The same truncation hit the 4.5 ms leader gap: (4,40) wants 529 outer steps and 529 & 0xFF is 17, so the gap came out 136 us -- and 136 us does not look like a truncated constant, it looks like a transmitter in a hurry. It now uses the (3,130) pair, whose 1064-clock step reaches the target in 255 steps. (2) THE BURSTS NEVER RE-DRIVE THE PIN. A gap is made by releasing the pad and a burst by driving it again, and the pin was driven once at the leader; the program then ran the whole frame -- eight bits, right gaps, right carrier, stop burst -- with the pad released for every burst except the first and last, emitting a leader, 13.6 ms of silence and one burst. Fixed by one shared ir_emit that every burst goes through, which is also the copy the duplication risk warned about. (3) A LADDER THAT DID NOT COVER ITS OWN INPUT DOMAIN: dmem[6] held 2/3/4 (the phase numbers, the obvious thing to write) and ir_burst_end treated anything past 1 as the stop burst, so the frame ended after the leader. Third instance in this block; the encoding is now 0/1/2 to match the ladder. (4) A SETUP BLOCK WITH NO ENTRY POINT: ir_send_byte sat after a JMP with nothing pointing at it, so the bit counter was never set and the loop ran 255 times instead of eight. (5) A BLOCK THAT FELL THROUGH INTO ITS OWN CALLER: ir_data_burst had no jump to the carrier and ran into ir_start_bit forever. And THREE IN THE TESTBENCH, which is the part worth recording: (6) $time is scaled to the module's timeunit (1 ns) and returns an integer, so timing a 13.1667 us half period with it quantises to 0.06 clocks and the carrier appeared to jitter 787/788/789 every sixteen cycles. Every width is now in TENTHS OF A NANOSECOND from $realtime -- a measuring instrument with 0.4 % quantisation cannot support a 0.06 % claim. (7) $rtoi rounds each edge independently, so a pair of touching pulses measures as -0.1 ns; one negative sample dragged the minimum down and then every other half period counted as an outlier against it, so a carrier silence is now bounded BELOW as well as above. (8) The burst reconstruction was a running accumulator that reported every burst as 22 carrier cycles while measuring its width correctly -- not a combination of numbers any waveform has. Rewritten as two passes, "which pulse starts each burst" then "where does each burst end", which removes the accumulator that was wrong. A "whole number of carrier cycles" check was written and then DELETED: the burst opens with a two-instruction initiation pulse, so its span is not a whole number of periods, and the check fired on all ten bursts including the correct ones. A check that is wrong about what it measures is worse than no check. THE MUTATION GATE FOUND THE ACT'S OWN BLIND SPOT, which is the most valuable thing in this entry: the gate's first run had 3 survivors out of 11, and one of them -- putting BOTH half periods on the same delay pair -- produced a carrier of 788 clocks one way and 782 the other. 38.05 kHz against 37.88, 0.4 % asymmetric on EVERY edge, inside every frequency window a real receiver has, and it PASSED: "each half is constant" is not "the two halves are equal". The TB now checks the two distributions against each other to within a clock, and that check is the act's whole point. The other two survivors were a burst of 20 carrier cycles (526 us, -6.4 %, which the old ±10 % window accepted -- now ±5 %, which admits the two widths a whole number of cycles can produce, 21 at -1.7 % and 22 at +2.9 %, and rejects the third) and a mutant that was behaviourally a no-op because I wrote its anchor against the byte's load rather than its rotate. GATE: mutate_timing_tb.sh goes 26 -> 37 cases, ALL 37 detected, 0 survived, 0 harness errors, firmware tree cmp-verified byte-identical after the run. FULL SUITE: run_all.sh --fast -j8 -> RTL 42/42, FIRMWARE 35/35, lint clean, all 14 mutation suites OK.

The act is NOT in run_all.sh and NOT in run_firmware_tests.sh, deliberately: wiring an act that fails would break the suite, and the DS18B20 WIP I inherited was honest about the same thing. WHAT IS VERIFIED: the ramp is exact on 8 of its 10 step intervals, measured as an EQUALITY against 5110 clocks and not a tolerance — periods 1661.133 us falling to 809.501 us (602 Hz to 1235 Hz), 12 step pulses, 12 distinct periods, all strictly shortening, all in band, dmem[14]=1, the firmware's own ramp counter landing on 76 after 12 decrements of 10. The measured numbers are the ones the act claims: the ramp is a subtraction (dmem[4] -= 10) rather than a table of twelve constants, so the linearity is in the program instead of being an assumption. TWO FAULTS OPEN, both stated rather than smoothed over. (1) TWO INTERVALS AROUND THE DIRECTION CHANGE ARE OFF BY A MATCHED PAIR: interval 4->5 measures 4736.71 clocks and 5->6 measures 5482.67, summing to exactly 2 x 5109.7 — so ~373 clocks of cost have moved one interval later than they belong. 373 clocks is close to ST_SETUP's 418, and the direction change plus its setup delay is the only thing in the program that is not a step, so the mechanism is almost certainly the setup delay landing in the interval after the one it should be in; NOT ROOT-CAUSED, and I am not going to guess in a record. (2) THE DIRECTION-CHANGE SETUP-TIME CHECK IS NOT RUNNING. The TB records two edges on DIR and searches every one of them for an interval that contains it, and finds none — so the setup-time assertion never executes. Its first version searched only the FIRST edge, which is the pin-matrix init rather than the flip, so it was vacuous by construction; fixing that exposed that the flip's timestamp is not landing where the search expects, which is fault (2) and is not the same as fault (1). TWO DEFECTS IN THE TESTBENCH THAT THE PROJECT HAS NOW SEEN TWICE, both recorded: $time is scaled to the module's timeunit and returns an integer, so every width here is in TENTHS OF A NANOSECOND from $realtime — this act's claim is that a step period is right to the CLOCK, and 1 ns quantisation is 0.06 clocks, an order of magnitude coarser than the claim. And the ramp was first computed in ONE pass that compared each period against the next BEFORE computing it, so every "ramp" equalled a period and every ramp check passed; it is now two loops, all the periods first and then all the differences, and a check that reads a value the loop has not filled in yet is a check that cannot fail while looking like a measurement in the output. RESUME POINT: root-cause the 373-clock displacement around the direction change (the phase-3 setup return is the first place to look), then the DIR edge placement, then wire into run_all.sh + run_firmware_tests.sh and add the mutation gate. One firmware defect already found and fixed on the way, and it is the DS18B20 lesson repeated verbatim: the ramp counter was dmem[9], which is the DELAY ROUTINE's own outer counter and counts itself down to zero, so the program ran twelve steps at a constant 2.09 ms — every step read back zero, subtracted ten, and got 246 again, the unsigned wrap, which is a perfectly plausible step period. A delay routine and its caller must never share a data slot. A second, and it is the same shape as the WS2812 byte counter: the ramp decremented BEFORE its first use, so the nominal 196 was spent immediately and the first interval was 85 us LONG — exactly the ramp step, which is what a counter initialised off by one looks like from the outside. A ramp counter is decremented after the value it selects has been used.

The DIR bug was the FOURTH write in the program that cleared the other pin, all with one cause: PINOE and TXPIN are whole registers, so a write meaning "this pin" is a write that also meaning "the other pin". Found in order: the step's level write (DIR zeroed by the first step), then ph1's release (DIR floating for every pulse), then st_step's PINOE (DIR released exactly when a driver samples it on the STEP edge -- hence the new ST_BOTH constant), and the park's. Fixed by keeping the direction VALUE in dmem[0] and carrying it in every write. The mutation gate found the act's own blind spot twice more: (a) a released DIR pad SURVIVED every edge-based check, because the edges it makes land on interval BOUNDARIES and a floating pad still reads as a valid level -- so the TB now measures the direction AT EVERY STEP EDGE, which is where a driver actually decodes it (first run 6 of 6 high, second 0 of 6 high, changing once at step 6); (b) st-dir-released survived TWICE and looked like a gap both times, and was neither -- it is a benign mutant, the pad is released for four instructions before the direction is set to the pull-down's own value, so the wire is identical. It was DELETED and replaced rather than caught by a loosened check. Gate 37 -> 48 cases, ALL 48 detected, 0 survived, firmware tree cmp-verified after the run. 2026-09-25 20:05 CDT | fw-timing | CHECKPOINT | BLOCK 3 DESIGN, before any code. The ISA constraint that shapes all three acts: 8-bit machine, NO CARRY FLAG (only JZ/JNZ exist), so 16-bit arithmetic must be hand-built. A 16-bit INCREMENT is cheap because +1 to the low byte is exactly zero when it carries; a 16-bit ADD of a variable needs an explicit carry test. DECIDED for (a) HC-SR04: capture the echo with the 1 us tick (STATUS bit0, clear-on-read), so the width is edge-detected on the pad and quantised to whole microseconds - the task asks for us resolution and 1 us is 0.17 mm, so this is the right instrument rather than a compromise; the counter is 16-bit in dmem[3]:dmem[2]. The distance conversion mm = us*11/64 (11/64 = 0.171875 vs the exact 0.1715, +0.2 %, stated) is the hard part: 16x16 multiply on a machine with no carry, needing either repeated addition with a count-down carry test or a bit-loop add. 8-bit arithmetic alone is NOT available for this, so it is real code and must be written carefully rather than skipped. DECIDED to start with (b) the frequency + duty meter instead, because its firmware is CAPTURE ONLY - two 16-bit tick counts, no conversion - so it is completable inside what is left of this context, and its TB (which does the ratio arithmetic) carries the 1 % claim. A half-verified ranging act would be worth less than one verified meter.

2026-09-25 21:40 CDT | fw-timing | TASK-DONE | BLOCK 3 ACT (b) INPUT FREQUENCY + DUTY METER GREEN ON REAL RTL AND WIRED IN. firmware/freqmeter.pe (103 words) + tb/tb_pe_soc_freqmeter.v + 3 peasm CONSTS (FM_IN, FM_PER_BASE, FM_HI_BASE). The act is CAPTURE ONLY and that is a conclusion, not a plan: the first design put a duty-percent divide in the firmware, and writing the ISA's 16-bit borrow test out properly (the borrow of a-b cannot be read off the difference - (a-b) mod 256 has a positive and a negative preimage for every value but zero, so it needs a four-case analysis on bit 7, and a COMPARE is the same analysis again) showed the divide would cost ~80 instructions of the subtlest code in the repository for a DISPLAY number. THE DESIGN THAT REMOVES IT: THE PERIOD IS T AT THE RISING EDGE BECAUSE T IS RESET AT EVERY RISING EDGE, and the high time is H at the rising edge for the same reason. Two resets delete every subtract, every compare and every divide, and they buy a second property: the counters are elapsed-since-the-last-edge rather than a difference of two absolute times, so a tick the program failed to notice cancels out of both ends of the measurement. The TB computes the duty as a percentage from the two 16-bit counts the firmware banks, and that claim - the RATIO is right even when both counts are a microsecond out - is exactly what the RED TB's header argued for. THE TIMEBASE TRAP, and it is the one that cost the most time: THIS SOC HAS TWO TICK COUNTERS AND THEY ARE NOT THE SAME SIZE. Port 7 STATUS is the UART HALF-BIT tick, 260 clocks = 4.33 us, because it exists to place a serial sample mid-cell; port 4 I2CTICK is the free-running 1 us counter. The first version counted STATUS and measured 0.24 ticks per microsecond - a factor of 4.33 out, SHORT at every point, reporting a 200 us period as 46 - and the only symptom was a watchdog. The fix is not to use the 1 us FLAG either: a clear-on-read flag is ONE bit, so a poll loop longer than a tick reports several ticks as one and the loss is invisible in the result. The program reads the COUNTER and adds the DIFFERENCE, elapsed = (now - previous) mod 256, exact for any loop under 255 us and this loop is 1.3 us. Two properties for three instructions: no flag to lose, so a slow iteration is late by exactly the right amount; and the borrow of the same subtraction is used only as "is it zero", the one comparison the ISA has, so ONE SUB does both jobs. And the add-of-a-variable carry test loses its both-zero guard for free, because the addend is the elapsed count and is never zero - the guard is not skipped, it is unnecessary. MEASURED, twelve banked points from 158 Hz to 10 kHz with a varying duty, each checked against a SECOND INDEPENDENT measurement of the pad by the TB's own receiver rather than against the generator's table (a generator that knew the answer would agree with a firmware that had the sweep wrong): 8000/10001/3150/4000/1600/2000/801/1000/400/500/80/100 us, high times 6000/2500/1039/400/1056/1600/480/399/180/275/12/50 us, duties 75.0/25.0/33.0/10.0/66.0/80.0/59.9/39.9/45.0/55.0/15.0/50.0 %. Every period is exact but three, by exactly ONE tick (10001, 801, 399 against 10000, 800, 400), which is the instrument's resolution showing up in the log instead of hiding in a tolerance. The 16-bit claim is point 1: 10001 us from a counter whose low byte wrapped 39 times; a byte-counter firmware reports 16 us there and has no symptom anywhere else. THE MACHINE HAS SIXTEEN BYTES OF DMEM and that is the constraint the RED TB broke: it asked for sixteen points in dmem[0..63] on a machine with 16 bytes, read twelve of its own expectations out of an address space that does not exist, and put its DONE flag at dmem[14] INSIDE the data it was reporting. A test that sizes memory to what it wishes for is a test that reports success without measuring anything - third such element in this block. So the sweep is SIX RUNS OF THREE PERIODS, the first of each run a warm-up that is not checked (the firmware starts mid-period, so the time since the previous rising edge is not a period - a receiver that starts mid-period misses that one period, and a TB that pretended otherwise would ask the firmware to measure something it was never present for). The map uses all sixteen and one byte is SHARED rather than found: the slot index doubles as the "no rising edge seen yet" state, because the program needed one byte more than the machine has. FOUR TESTBENCH DEFECTS, which is the part worth recording. (1) pin_in_bus = {1'b1, 1'b1, pwm, 5'b11111} put the PWM ON BIT 5, not 6 - eight bits with pwm third from the top - so the firmware read a pad that never changed and sat polling for 60 ms. Inherited from my own RED TB; the three sibling acts all write the same concatenation correctly, so the right form was in the repository and was not copied. (2) The receiver was armed when the pad was PRESENTED rather than when the firmware was RELEASED, so whether the presentation was itself an edge depended on where the previous run's waveform stopped: two of the twelve points were compared against the wrong interval, and it was off by one exactly when nobody was looking. (3) `if (a > b ? w : f)` PARSES AS if ((a > b) ? w : f) and both arms are nonzero, so the condition was ALWAYS TRUE: the period and high-time checks reported "12 of 12 are not" for values differing by 0.0000 us. The identical expression in a $display evaluated correctly, which is why it took a debug copy with the difference printed to find it. (4) A result that was NEVER WRITTEN passed every arithmetic check, because a comparison against an unknown is false in Verilog - found by asking what a finishes-after-one-point mutant would do BEFORE writing the mutant, and the TB now has an explicit "was it actually written" check. MUTATION GATE: 48 -> 58 cases. The first run of the ten new ones found three defects IN MY OWN MUTANT DEFINITIONS and one more in the gate's contract, which is the second time this gate has paid for itself. (a) Two anchors did not match (the label lines carry trailing comments, so an anchor beginning at a label never matches) and two --const cases had NO anchor at all, which run_case's python step treats as a harness error rather than a mutation - so half the new cases were never run and the harness said so loudly rather than reporting a pass. (b) fm-idx-stuck's replacement INSERTED a NOP instead of replacing the store, so the mutant changed nothing and survived. (c) fm-high-is-period survived and is NOT A DEFECT: at the falling edge the period counter and the high-time counter hold the SAME number, because both are reset at the rising edge and both advance on every elapsed microsecond, so banking the high time from either counter is the same program. DELETED and replaced by fm-high-byte-order (the pair banked high byte first, which reads as a real measurement times 256 rather than as an addressing fault) - the third time in this block that a surviving mutant turned out to be a no-op rather than a gate that needed loosening. COST, stated: 43 ms of 60 MHz, 2.6 M clocks, the largest simulation in the repository, 55 s of wall. Not trimmed: the 100 Hz point is where the 16-bit claim is tested. Two things were done about it and neither weakened a claim - the generator is EDGE-DRIVEN with absolute delays rather than woken once per clock (a 2.6 M clock TB that woke 2.6 M times to decide whether the pin had moved would spend its wall time deciding it had not), and DONE is polled every 256 clocks rather than every clock because the firmware is parked once it is set and a per-clock read of dut.dmem is 2.6 M hierarchical reads. SAME-LIST: the TB is in run_all.sh, the firmware in run_firmware_tests.sh.

The peasm.py difference the manager saw is MY OWN UNCOMMITTED BLOCK-3 WIP (the three FM_* constants), which is now a separate commit d88e316. THE CAUSE: rtl/pe_cpu.v, where the R3 block added two MODULE INPUTS, dbg_hold and dbg_step. A testbench that predates them leaves the ports unconnected, the port arrives as Z, `!dbg_hold` is X, so `cpu_exec` is X, so `if (cpu_exec)` is false and THE CORE NEVER COMMITS AN INSTRUCTION: dmem reads back x, the pad never moves, zero edges, a watchdog. The evidence is a PATTERN, not an argument: of the nineteen TBs that instantiate pe_soc, exactly the SEVEN that do not tie those two ports are the seven that fail, and the twelve that do all pass. A merge that dropped content does not produce that shape; a new port with no default does, precisely. REPAIR as commit 3657847, two independent hunks so neither can mask the other: (1) rtl/pe_cpu.v tests both debug inputs with CASE equality against 1'b1, so anything that is not a hard one reads as "not held"/"no step" -- identical in synthesis (x and z do not exist in hardware), and the convention belongs at the CONSUMER because a call site is the one place that can forget it; (2) the six acts' TBs plus the frequency meter's tie the ports explicitly. EITHER ALONE IS SUFFICIENT: verified on throwaway `git archive main` exports (main worktree never touched) -- B: main unpatched 6/6 FAIL; A: main + the RTL hunk only 6/6 PASS; C: main + the six TB hunks only 6/6 PASS; worktree: main + both 6/6 PASS. THREE PROCESS NOTES, and the last two are the ones I would keep. (1) I broke my own repair ONCE, SILENTLY: the port list ended without a trailing comma, so the .dbg_hold line became part of the comment and the port was still unconnected -- the same defect as not writing it, and it elaborates and runs; all six still failed. Nothing but COMPILING caught it: no test, no gate, no assertion. An elaboration failure is the only instrument that sees a port that is syntactically fine and semantically absent. (2) The full suite here reached RTL 46/46 and FIRMWARE 37/37 -- with the frequency-meter act in and green -- and then eight mutation harnesses failed with "OSError: [Errno 28] No space left on device", one of which could not restore its snapshot ("FATAL: rtl/pe_eth_mac.v does not match the snapshot after restore"), leaving SIXTEEN FILES MUTATED in my worktree. That is the documented hazard reproduced by running out of disk: the per-worktree lock protects files from concurrent RUNS, not from a restore that fails. Recovery was `git checkout -- firmware/ rtl/ formal/`; the tree is clean at HEAD and mutate_eth_mac_tb.sh then reported 30 detected / 0 survived / 0 harness errors with the tree byte-identical afterwards, so those eight failures were environmental and not the repair. (3) I KILLED ANOTHER WORKER'S RUN DOING IT: stopping my own suite with `pkill -9 -f "[v]vp"` matched the MANAGER'S OWN verification run in /tmp/vm-red, which exited 137 and printed "MERGE GATE: RED -- DO NOT PUSH HEAD". The pattern was self-match-proof but not SCOPE-proof: a pattern that cannot match itself can still match everyone else's processes. pkill is the wrong instrument in a shared /tmp; kill by PID, by named process tree.

2026-09-25 23:40 CDT | fw-timing | TASK-START | BLOCK 3 ACT (a) HC-SR04 ULTRASONIC RANGING. firmware/sr04_range.pe + tb/tb_pe_soc_sr04.v: a 10 us trigger pulse, the echo pulse width captured to 1 us resolution, and the width converted to millimetres on a machine with no carry flag. THE CONVERSION IS THE ACT. mm = us * 11/64, and the 20:05 note said this needs a 16x16 multiply with an explicit carry test, +0.2 % against exact. TWO IMPROVEMENTS ON THAT NOTE, both from what act (b) taught. FIRST, THE MULTIPLY IS NOT NEEDED: mm = us*11/64 with us = 64q + r is EXACTLY 11q + floor(11r/64), so it is a multiply by 11 of a 10-bit value and a multiply by 11 of a 6-bit value plus one add -- no 16x16 anything, and the +0.2 % is a property of the CONSTANT 11/64 (0.171875 against the speed of sound's 0.17150), not of the arithmetic, which is now exact for the constant it is given. SECOND, the multiply by 11 needs no loop and no multiply instruction: 11 = 8 + 2 + 1, so it is three in-place 16-bit DOUBLINGS and two adds, and a doubling's carry is BIT 7 OF THE ORIGINAL LOW BYTE -- one AND with 0x80 and a JNZ -- which is the cheapest carry test in this ISA and unlike a general add's it needs NO both-zero guard, because doubling zero carries nothing and the sum is zero only in that case. So the whole conversion is ~70 instructions of fixed addresses and NO shared data slot: the 16 bytes are T(0,1) US(2,3) PREV(4) STATE(5) slot0 mm(6,7) slot1 mm(8,9) N(10) DONE(11) Q(12,13) ACC(14,15), and the term-2 accumulator is T reused AFTER the falling edge has latched the width into US -- which is safe because the next echo edge is milliseconds away and T is dead until then. Two measurements per run for the same reason act (b) was two points per run: THE MACHINE HAS SIXTEEN BYTES. The trigger is a COUNTED delay in peasm's CONSTS (a fitted instruction count, so the mutation gate reaches it with --const), the echo is captured with the same 1 us I2CTICK-difference shape act (b) proved, and the pads are TRIG 0x40 (driven) + ECHO 0x20 (released) with PINOE written with BOTH bits -- the fourth write in this repository that cleared the other pin is the reason that is a constant here.

(5) The conversion's HIGH BYTE was shifted TWICE where a 16-bit >>6 needs SIX, making Q 274 instead of 18 and turning a 1160 us echo into 822 mm. THE BOTH-ZERO GUARD IN A GENERAL 16-BIT ADD IS NOT EXACT, and it is wrong in the way that matters: it is derived from a carry test that is exact for a DOUBLING and silently wrong for an ADD. The guard's rule is "if the stored low byte is ZERO, re-read the addend and see whether it was zero too; otherwise there was no carry" -- and the second half of that rule is FALSE. A carry out of the low byte does not require the low result to be zero: 0xE0 + 0x38 = 0x118, which wraps, carries, and leaves 0x18, and 0x18 is not zero, so the guard takes the "no carry" branch and the high byte is never incremented. THE CHAIN, TRACED WITH THE FAULT'S OWN NUMBERS, IS ENOUGH: the small term for r=56 is 11*56 = 616 = 0x0268, and the answer 991 implies a second term of 1, which is 0x0068 after the >>6 -- a high byte of 0 where there should be 2. The chain is T=56 (0x0038) -> double 112 (0x0070) -> double 224 (0x00E0) -> += 56: the low byte computes 0xE0+0x38 = 0x118, the guard sees 0x18, concludes "no carry", and the high byte stays 0 where it must become 1. Everything after that is arithmetic on a wrong value, which is why the error is 199 - 208 rather than something dramatic: the two lost high-byte units and the two doublings that follow turn one lost carry into a small plausible-looking number. WHY IT SURVIVED THE FIRST MEASUREMENT: 1160 & 63 = 8, and 8 -> 16 -> 32 -> 40 never carries in the low byte (0x20 + 0x08 = 0x28), so the same flawed guard is CORRECT for that value and the first measurement came out EXACT. A carry test that is exact for one operand range and wrong for another is the worst kind: it is not a defect the first measurement can find. THE PRECISE STATEMENT OF THE LESSON, and it belongs beside the ISA notes: THE CARRY OUT OF A DOUBLING IS BIT 7 OF THE ORIGINAL LOW BYTE, and the carry out of a general ADD IS A FACT ABOUT THE SUM -- TWO DIFFERENT FACTS, and a test derived from one is not a test of the other. The zero-result test IS exact for a 16-bit INCREMENT (because +1 can only wrap to zero) and for a doubling (because the test used is bit 7, not the result); it is exact for NOTHING else. THE FIX, not yet applied, and it is the standard identity rather than a cleverer zero test: carry = ((xl & yl) | ((xl | yl) & ~sum)) with sum = xl + yl mod 256 and ~sum = 255 - sum -- about ten extra instructions at each of the three general-add sites, all of which this ISA has. A cheaper-looking alternative I rejected: making the chain avoid general adds altogether, which would mean a different multiply by 11 (11 = 8+2+1 still needs two adds of variables, and repeated doubling cannot reach 11), so the identity is the fix. ALSO WORTH RECORDING: the frequency meter is NOT affected, because it uses only 16-bit INCREMENTS, whose carry test is exact -- so the same guard, written for the wrong operation, is harmless in one act and fatal in another, and neither firmware has any way to tell the reader which is which except the comment.

2026-09-26 13:05 CDT | fw-timing | TASK-START | BLOCK 3 ACT (c) FM0/FM1 BI-PHASE, ENCODE AND DECODE THROUGH THE PINS IN LOOPBACK. Starting the last act of the block, and it is independent of the blocked HC-SR04 edit, so the block is not stalled by it. THE PROTOCOL, from the wire rules and not from a datasheet I would have to trust: a bi-phase-coded bit is TWO half-intervals and the CODE LIVES IN THE TRANSITIONS, not in the levels. A '1' has NO transition at the end of its interval in both encodings; a '0' has a transition at the end. FM0 adds a transition at the START of the interval for a '1', and FM1 does not -- so the two encodings of the same data are distinguishable ONLY by whether a transition appears at the interval boundary, which is the whole reason a receiver can recover the clock from the stream at all. CONSEQUENCES THE DESIGN HAS TO RESPOND TO, and they are the reason this act is worth doing: (1) THE RECEIVER MUST RECOVER THE CLOCK FROM THE TRANSITIONS, because a loopback through a pad carries no clock at all -- the firmware's own 1 us counter is not the bit clock, and using it would be testing a synchroniser against a source it shares a clock with; (2) A STREAM WITH NO TRANSITIONS IS NOT A VALID FRAME -- a run of ones in FM1 is a constant level -- so the act's interesting failures are the ones a synchroniser makes silently: it locks onto a DATA TRANSITION and comes out half a frame out, which is a right-looking answer; (3) THE TWO ENCODINGS DIFFER ONLY AT INTERVAL STARTS, so a decoder that gets the boundary phase wrong decodes FM0 as FM1 and produces a plausible byte that is not the one sent, and the only way to tell is to check BOTH encodings against the SAME data and require the receiver to declare which one it locked onto. THE ACT'S SHAPE, both directions, because the dispatch says encode AND decode: the firmware ENCODES a known byte on a pad with a counted bit period, and a Verilog decoder in the testbench -- written from the wire rules above, not from the firmware -- recovers it and it must match byte for byte; and the testbench ENCODES a byte on an input pad, in a framing the firmware's decoder recovers, and the two must agree. Loopback in the sense that matters: neither side is told what the other sent, and the firmware's decoder is written from the same wire rules as the testbench's. MEMORY: the machine has sixteen bytes, so a frame's worth of state (the recovered byte, the bit counter, the transition-time snapshot, the phase flag) is most of the map, and the encode side wants a table of a few bytes -- which means the act measures TWO OR THREE BYTES and not eight, and the header must say so. THE FIRST THING TO BUILD IS THE TESTBENCH, RED, because every act in this block has had its most expensive defect found by the instrument and not by the design, and the FM0/FM1 receiver is exactly the kind of thing whose decoder looks right until it is decoded by something that was written from the same misunderstanding. The single most valuable check in it will be the one this protocol is named for: the testbench's decoder and the firmware's decoder must be written from the WIRE RULES and not from each other, so that a shared misreading of "a '1' has no transition at the end" fails in both directions at once.
2026-09-26 13:20 CDT | fw-timing | TASK-WIP | BLOCK 3 ACT (c) FM0/FM1, and the FIRST ISA CONSTRAINT this act has found, found by asking the ISA a question the act actually needs answered rather than by writing code and reading a failure. A self-synchronising bi-phase receiver does not synchronise on a pin level: it TIMESTAMPS the level, and on every tick compares the level with the level it recorded at the last change. That single idiom -- remember where the level last CHANGED, and compare against that rather than against the previous sample -- is what recovers the clock from the data, and it is the whole reason a loopback with no shared clock works at all. On this machine it is NOT IN THE INSTRUCTION SET. OP_LDS (0xB) is `a <= ram[x]`: it loads the ACCUMULATOR from the indexed address, and there is no encoding that puts the result in X. So `X = ram[X]`, the store-forward a transition detector wants, cannot be written, and the encoding that LOOKS like it -- `LDM X, addr` with arg[7]=1 -- is a DIRECT load whose address field is the top nibble only, so it cannot even name byte 8. The consequence for this act is specific and worth stating before any code is written: a transition detector on this machine has to BOUNCE THROUGH A SCRATCH BYTE (read the indexed byte, keep the old level in a memory location, compare, store), and a scratch byte is a data slot, which is the hazard this block has now hit in five separate acts -- a delay routine and its caller must never share a data slot, and neither must a decoder and its sample history. So the act's memory map has to say which bytes belong to the transition history and which to the frame being assembled, and the testbench's decoder, which has no such constraint, is free to use registers: WHICH MEANS THE TWO DECODERS WILL NOT BE SYMMETRIC, and the testbench's decoder is the one that gets to be naive. That asymmetry is an argument for writing the firmware's decoder FIRST and then checking the testbench's decoder against it on a stream where the two must agree, rather than the reverse. IT ALSO SUGGESTS THE ACT'S MORE INTERESTING SHAPE: the interesting claim is not "the firmware decodes a byte" but "the firmware recovers the CLOCK from the data and says which encoding it locked onto", and a decoder that cannot fail is not a decoder. The first check in the testbench should therefore be a stream that CANNOT be decoded -- a run of ones in FM1, which is a constant level with no transitions at all -- and the firmware's answer to it must be a declared failure rather than a byte it made up. The act is otherwise unchanged from the 13:05 entry: encode on one pad with a testbench decoder written from the wire rules, decode a testbench-encoded stream, and require both directions to agree with neither side told what the other sent. NOT in run_all.sh, NOT in run_firmware_tests.sh, and the testbench does not exist yet -- this is a design record, and the RED testbench is the next thing to build.

THE WIRE RULES, WHICH I WROTE AT THE TOP OF THE SAME FILE, SAY THE OPPOSITE: the transition difference is at the END of the interval, which is only meaningful if the level ALSO changes WITHIN the interval -- that is what "bi-phase" means. So the correct FM1 encoding is first half = the complement of the data and second half = the data, which makes EVERY bit carry a transition in its middle; a '0' then carries a second one at the bit boundary and a '1' does not. WHICH MEANS MY HEADLINE CLAIM IS WRONG TOO: "a run of ones in FM1 is a constant level with no transitions at all" is FALSE. A run of ones in FM1 is a clean square wave, every bit transitioning in its middle, and the decoder recovers the clock from exactly those. There is no well-formed frame with no transitions, so the testbench's last check -- "a frame of all ones in FM1 is a CONSTANT LEVEL and the receiver must DECLARE that" -- is asserting a property of the PROTOCOL that does not exist, and it would have driven the firmware into inventing a failure case to satisfy it. WHAT THE REAL UNDECODABLE STREAM IS, and it is worth getting right because it is the act's best check: a line that is CONSTANT -- stuck at one level, which is what a disconnected, shorted or dead sensor looks like on the wire -- has no transitions at all and carries no clock, so a receiver that locks on to it has locked on to nothing. That is the stream to test, and it is a pad held at a level rather than a frame of ones. WHAT I WROTE, at the top of the testbench and at the top of the firmware: "a '1' has NO transition at the END of its interval; a '0' HAS one." THAT IS NOT BIPHASE CODING. It is a description of a scheme in which the data lives at the bit BOUNDARY, and working from it I derived an encoder whose '1' was a constant level, then "fixed" it into one that satisfies my wrong rules rather than the protocol. THE REAL RULES, which I should have written down from the coding rather than from a half-remembered summary: EVERY BIT CARRIES A TRANSITION IN ITS MIDDLE -- always, for both encodings, which is what makes the stream self-clocking and is the entire reason the encoding exists; THE DATA IS THE LEVEL OF THE FIRST HALF-INTERVAL; and FM1 versus FM0 is simply WHICH LEVEL MARKS A ONE at the start of the interval -- FM1 marks a one LOW and FM0 marks a one HIGH. From that: a bit boundary carries a transition if and only if two ADJACENT BITS DIFFER, so a run of identical bits has NO boundary transitions at all, and a run of ones is a clean square wave. MY "a '1' has no transition at the end" is the same error one place over: the thing that is absent for a one is the transition at the START, not at the end, and the mid-bit transition is never absent. THE CONSEQUENCE THAT RESHAPES THE ACT, and it is the reason I am writing this down rather than coding: A BIPHASE STREAM CARRIES NO POLARITY INFORMATION. Nothing in the waveform says whether a one is the low first half or the high first half, and that is not a detail of the receiver, it is a property of the code: a receiver that guesses the polarity will decode a perfectly well-formed frame into its complement, and every byte will be wrong by a consistent bit inversion, which is the most plausible-looking failure in the whole act. SO THE PROTOCOL NEEDS A PREAMBLE, and "the receiver declares WHICH ENCODING it locked onto" is only a meaningful check if the frame STARTS with something that MARKS the polarity -- a sync pattern, or a break, or an inverted sync. My testbench's flag check was meaningful only because I had not thought about where the polarity comes from, and the flag would have been the receiver reporting a GUESS. THE ACT AS IT SHOULD BE BUILT, and this is the design to implement next: the frame opens with a preamble that is asymmetric -- a pattern whose level in the first half cannot be produced by the other encoding's reading of the same pattern -- the receiver locks its polarity on the preamble, and only then decodes data; and the check that matters most is that the SAME frame, sent as FM0 and as FM1, produces two different flag values and the same three bytes, which is the only way to prove the flag is measured rather than assumed. The testbench's encoder, the firmware's decoder, and the header's three copies of the wrong rules all need rewriting against these; nothing else in the act changes, and the testbench's checks -- both directions, the flag, and the constant-line case -- are the right checks for the right protocol.

=== BLOCK 3 PROGRESS ===

(b) THE INPUT FREQUENCY AND DUTY METER IS GREEN AND IN THE REGRESSION (d88e316). Twelve banked points from 158 Hz to 10 kHz, each checked against a SECOND INDEPENDENT measurement of the pad rather than against the generator's table, the tolerance stated per point because the instrument's resolution IS the claim, 58/58 mutations. Wired into run_all.sh and run_firmware_tests.sh.

(a) HC-SR04 RANGING: THE CONVERSION IS EXACT -- 1160 us -> 199 mm against (1160*11)/64 = 199 -- and the trigger is 601 clocks = 10.017 us as an EQUALITY against a derivation (4*SR_TRIG + 5) counted from the assembler's listing rather than asserted. The exact carry is verified for all 65,536 operand pairs in two forms (four temporaries and three); the three-temporary form ships, with the temporaries at dmem[6],dmem[7],dmem[11] and the answer banked low byte first. THE ONE OPEN ITEM IS THE TESTBENCH, and the recipe is in the 23:45 line above: restructure tb_pe_soc_sr04.v into two runs, looping over the two distances, resetting between (rst_n low, reload, four stopped clocks then #1), and reading the answer AT THE MOMENT dmem[10] goes to 1 rather than at the end of the run -- the end-reading is what reported a correct conversion as 222 mm. Also open: three stray READS in the leftover acc_x11_h tail, which the width-byte rule catches (no instruction outside init / echo_down / convert may touch dmem[2] or dmem[3], because those two bytes ARE the width and the width is the measurement).

(c) FM0/FM1 BI-PHASE: a RED testbench whose seven checks are RIGHT, a first-draft firmware, and the WIRE RULES CORRECTED -- every bit carries a transition in its middle, the data is the level of the first half-interval, FM1 versus FM0 is which level marks a one, and therefore A BIPHASE STREAM CARRIES NO POLARITY INFORMATION. The act needs a preamble: an asymmetric pattern whose first-half level the other encoding's reading cannot produce, so the receiver locks polarity before it decodes. THE CHECK THAT MATTERS MOST: the same frame sent as FM0 and as FM1 must give two different flag values and the same three bytes, which is the only way to prove the flag is measured rather than assumed. The testbench's encoder also had to be corrected -- it was not bi-phase at all, making a 1 a constant level -- and a header claim in four places that a run of ones in FM1 is undecodable was FALSE: it is a clean square wave. The real undecodable stream is a line held CONSTANT, which is what a dead sensor looks like.

  * tools/fw/peasm.py -- the (2,13) pair is 69 clocks = 1.15 us, not 1.22 (cacaf23)
  * spi_mode3.pe -- the WORD LIST is 0x1134, 0x2245, 0x3356, because line 224 adds the index (0, 17, 34) to BOTH bytes as independent 8-bit adds with no carry into the high byte (4a7e172)

NOT MINE, AND ALREADY DONE: firmware/dmx512.pe and the spi_mode3.pe RESPONSE line are not on my branch. Commit 1798abf on fw-bus-protocols, "docs(firmware): correct two header figures the wire contradicts", already corrects both. I reverted my own edit of the spi_mode3 response line (222f384) precisely BECAUSE theirs is better -- it derives the wire byte as the high byte XOR 0x7E -- so my branch still carries the stale response list and theirs replaces it cleanly.

THE ONE ITEM NEEDING TWO WORKERS TO AGREE, AND IT IS ARITHMETIC RATHER THAN JUDGEMENT: 1798abf states response bytes 0x6D, 0x6C, 0x6F, which is what the high bytes 0x11, 0x12, 0x13 give -- the OLD word list. With the corrected words the high bytes are 0x11, 0x22, 0x33 and the wire bytes are 0x6F, 0x5C, 0x4D. So my word-list fix INVALIDATES their response figures, and whichever commit lands second has to recompute them. I flagged it rather than editing their line.

2026-09-25 20:49 CDT | fw-bus | CORRECTION | my "run-lock is load-sensitive" was wrong, and the real diagnosis is better. It is ALWAYS the same sub-check: "E: a cleanly finished run left its child running" -- in both suite runs AND standalone just now (15 passed, 1 failed). Earlier I ran it 3x standalone and got 16/16, so it is genuinely intermittent, but load is NOT the discriminator: it fails with nothing else contending. E runs the runner with "exit 0", waits for it, sleeps 0.4s, and asserts the child is gone; the child is a "while :; do echo x >> heartbeat; sleep 0.05; done" loop. So this is an intermittent defect in the clean-EXIT cleanup path of regress/run_lock.sh: sometimes a cleanly finished run does leave its child running -- which is exactly the failure mode the lock exists to prevent, reported by the lock-s own test.

2026-09-25 23:54 CDT | pw-diag-timing | FINDING | the gate was GREEN on D3, and the reason is the general one. The conversion audit matched "69 clocks = 1.22 us" and a wiki page writes the same fact as an APPOSITIVE - "a 69-clock (1.22 us) step". The connective between count and value was mandatory (= / is / of), so the FIGURES were covered and the PAGES were not. A gate that covers the diagrams and misses the pages is a gate that is green precisely where a reader is most likely to quote from. Fixed: connective optional, parenthetical allowed, and the conversion negative control now uses the APPOSITIVE form - a control written in the "=" form would have kept passing while the hole was open. Widening a pattern is not free and produced two false findings, both caught by the gate: "788.95 clocks" matched as "95 clocks" (reported the NEC figure's own correct 13.149 us as an 11.6 us error) and "24 x 75 clocks" matched as "75 clocks" (that is 1800). A gate that reports false findings gets its tolerance loosened by the next reader and then reports nothing; both fixed with lookbehinds. Now 38 conversions recomputed clean across 20 files, both negative controls tripping, and the only failure is the 12 undrawn files the gate NAMES rather than skips.

The real rule, derived from source: `logic [7:0] sl_resp` means the mask's low half 0x5A is NEVER COMPUTED, so it is `resp = word[15:8] XOR 0x7E`, one line, one byte. The claim survives and is stronger (the response depends on the RECEIVED high byte). Page + figure fixed; the measured bytes were never in question.

That premise is FALSE for all sixteen harnesses: they restore PER CASE inside the mutation loop and then immediately apply the next mutant, so a clean run's own content sequence is M O M O M O. The proposed signal - mutated->original followed by further mutation - is therefore the DOMINANT PATTERN of normal operation, not a signature of interference. Measured on the real suites with a 2ms content-class recorder: serdes 6 pristine windows of 13-18ms, eth_tx 17 windows of 13-32ms, and i2c windows of 60-62ms which are LONGER than the design's own 50ms poll. Shipping it would return exit 4 from every suite and make verify_merge permanently INCONCLUSIVE, which is worse than the gap it closes: an INCONCLUSIVE that always fires is one people learn to ignore. Not shipped. Evidence and the two sound replacements in reviews/2026-09-26/DEP-GUARD-SAMPLER-DESIGN.md sections 6-7; the disproof is now case 11 of regress/test_dep_guard.sh, executable and green (6 false hits on a clean 3-case run), so the next implementer cannot re-derive it trusting the prose.

The probe was wrong: its multi-file version reset the seen/back state INSIDE the per-poll loop, so it compared one sample against itself and could never fire - a guard never seen to fire, which is not evidence of anything. ## 2026-09-27 act (c): the phase is fixed BY THE MODEL, and both polarities
## now give the same three bytes with two different flags

**The model came first, as the handoff asked.** `/tmp/fm_model.py` is written
from the wire rules and from nothing else in this repository: it builds the
level sequence for both polarities, lists the transition times and the interval
sequence, and runs the receiver algorithm. It reproduces the measured fault
exactly -- 39 emitted bits, sixteen ones, then the frame delayed by one bit,
for the FM0 stream -- so it is a model of THIS firmware and not of an idea.

**What the model says about the handoff's own claim.** The handoff says that
under FM1 "there is no frame-start transition and nothing is lost". The model
says FM1 loses a bit too: its first transition is the MID of bit 0, the old
algorithm reads that as a boundary, and 31 payload bits come out, exactly as 31
come out for FM0. The phase error does not disappear under FM1; it moves to the
other side of the stream.

**The preamble SHAPE can be made polarity-independent, and it is not a
compensation.** The first transition of a transmission is the only one whose
class differs between the polarities (a boundary into bit 0 under FM0, the mid
of bit 0 under FM1), and the resync drops it in both cases. So the receiver
must not be told which case it is in. The fix is that it stops guessing:
`dmem[15]` becomes a THREE-state phase (1 = the last transition was a boundary,
0 = a mid, 2 = UNKNOWN), a one-half gap emits nothing while the state is 2, and
only a TWO-half gap may emit -- a two-half gap is mid-then-mid whatever came
before it, so it is the one interval in this encoding that is unambiguous on
its own. The preamble's eight-zero run guarantees exactly one such gap, and the
model puts it at t = 34 us of the frame in BOTH polarities. The eight ones
after it are then received whole, as the preamble's first byte, and their
LEVEL is the polarity: 0xFF is FM0, 0x00 is FM1. Both polarities lose the same
eight preamble bits, which is what lets one preamble-end test serve both.

    MEASURED, one frame, two polarities, reset between:
      pass 0: sent FM0 -> banked a5 3c 96, dmem[3] = 00
      pass 1: sent FM1 -> banked a5 3c 96, dmem[3] = 01

are compared against each other: two different flags and the same three bytes.

## 2026-09-27 act (c): the MANAGER RULES THE ENCODER SHAPE -- LOOPBACK

**The encoder sends THE THREE BANKED BYTES back (dmem[0..2]), re-encoded under
the encoding the decoder DETECTED, and the testbench's decoder decodes three
bytes and compares them against the frame that was sent. The same frame must
come back byte-identical under either encoding.** It is the act's name -- bi-
phase LOOPBACK -- and it is the only shape in which the flag is load-bearing in
BOTH directions.

**Why the ruling is sharp, and it needed no argument:** neither side is told the
other's polarity. The testbench's decoder locks its own from the preamble's
levels. So a firmware that re-encoded under the WRONG flag would still put three
recognisable bytes on the wire, and the testbench's decoder would invert them
with ITS OWN flag and hand back the complement. A wrong encoder polarity is
caught, and only because the receiver inverts with a polarity it measured
itself.

**Consequences, recorded so the next session does not rederive them.** The
return leg needs the SAME preamble and the SAME three-state phase lock the
decoder now has -- the first transition of a transmission is the one whose
class depends on the polarity, and the testbench's decoder resyncs on it exactly
as the firmware's did. 40 bits go out (16 preamble + 24 payload) = 160 us, which
the 1200 us per pass still covers. `dmem[6]` is free because the byte to send is
no longer a tx byte, and dmem[13] stops being shared: the stub counted
half-intervals in the DECODER's preamble byte. Nothing else is safe -- main
writes dmem[10] and dmem[14] on every poll, and "written and never read" is not
the same as free.

**AND THE ENTRY POINT IS A DECISION.** `frame_done` is where the three bytes
become available; main's `LDM A,11 / JNZ encoding` is where the encoder is
reached from today. Under loopback both cannot be the entry: an encoder entered
from the poll loop is re-entered by the next poll and restarts the
transmission. Either frame_done sets the mode byte and the dispatch is the one
entry, or frame_done jumps in and the dispatch is deleted. Do not leave both.

**`<<wip>>` until green.** Two red checks, both naming it.

### THE SHR FINDING, and it is the most expensive kind of stale figure

**This act's comments are wrong about the ISA in three places, and the error is
load-bearing for exactly the work that is left.** The firmware header, the
`NO DIVISION` block, and the previous handoff all say this machine has no
shift-right. It does. Assembled and counted, not read:

    0  D00C  LDM A, 12
    1  A000  SHR          <- opcode 0xA, a <= {1'b0, a[7:1]}, no operand
    4  A000  SHR A        <- "SHR A" is the same word; it is documentation

It shifts by exactly one and DISCARDS the bit it shifts out, so the encoder
tests the top bit first and only then peels. What this changes: the high-bit-
first wire order, adopted last session as the only free option, is free because
the encoder could not cheaply do the other thing -- and now it can do this one
too. **The claim must be corrected in the firmware's comments in the same
step**, because the next reader will design the encoder around a limitation
that does not exist. There is still no shift-LEFT, so the decoder's shift-in
stays a doubling and everything measured about it stands.

**A stale figure that is LOAD-BEARING is a different animal from the five this
block has already found.** Four of those were a number asserted in a second
place; this one is a claim about the machine itself, and acting on it produces
a design that cannot be built. The cure is the one that has worked every time
in this block: go to the assembler and the RTL and COUNT, rather than believing
the comment that explains the code.

## 2026-09-27 act (c): THE ENCODER TRANSMITS, AND THE DELAY LOOP WAS A RUNNING SUM

**The ruling is implemented to the model, the SHR finding is corrected in all
three places, and the encode direction is still red -- on the testbench's side,
with the firmware's wire now measured and explained.** `e2b8e75`, 300 words,
five checks green and a sixth added, four red checks where the handoff left
two.

**THE MODEL CAME FIRST, as the handoff ordered, and it is what the firmware was
written against.** `firmware/bmc_model.py` grows a `Tx` that builds the return
leg half-interval by half-interval from the wire rules in both polarities,
checks it against the wire rules interval for interval (80 of 80 each), and runs
the ROUND TRIP through its own receiver at three offsets and both idle levels.
The model's receiver comes back with `a5 3c 96` and the right flag every time,
so "the same frame byte-identical under either encoding" is a property of the
model before it is a property of silicon. It exits non-zero on a failure, so
the model is a gate and not a printout.

**TWO THINGS THE MODEL SETTLED THAT THE HANDOFF HAD WRONG.** The return leg's
idle level is LOW, not high -- the output latch is written to 0 by init -- so
the frame-start asymmetry FLIPS sides between the two legs: FM0 gets no
frame-start transition on the return leg and FM1 does, the opposite of the
input leg. The lock holds either way, because it is made by the preamble's
two-half gap and not by the level the line was resting at. And the encoder
must send NOTHING when `dmem[3] = 0xFF`, which is now a rule in the model and a
test in the firmware.

### THE SHR FINDING, CORRECTED IN ALL THREE PLACES IN THE SAME STEP

The header, the `NO DIVISION` block and the `LEFT SHIFT` comment now say what
the assembler says. The encoder USES it: the byte is held with the bit now
going at bit 7 and peeled one place per bit with SHR, so "which bit is this" is
not a question the program asks, and no mask is dispatched per boundary. There
is still no shift-LEFT, so the receiver's shift-in is still a doubling.

### THE ENTRY POINT IS frame_done, AND THE POLL LOOP'S DISPATCH IS DELETED

`LDM A,11 / JNZ encoding` is gone rather than left beside the new entry: two
entries to one transmitter re-enters it on the next poll and restarts the
transmission in the middle of it. `dmem[11]` was the mode byte and is now the
encoder's byte; `dmem[6]` is the half-interval counter and `dmem[13]` is the
decoder's alone, as ruled.

### THE 120-CLOCK HALF-INTERVAL, AND THE HANDOFF'S OWN RECIPE WAS WRONG

`firmware/bmc_checks.py` grows a **sixth check**: it walks every route from one
`OUT TXPIN` to the next in the assembler's listing and counts clocks. Proven
against three faults, each of which it caught:

| fault put back | what it reports |
| :--- | :--- |
| a wrong fitted constant | 124 clocks |
| a NOP pad removed | 119 clocks |
| the running-sum loop | 455 clocks |

**The handoff suggested `LDI A,1 / SUB A,X / JNZ` and "3 per iteration, so 40
is 120 clocks". It is three instructions and it does not count.** SUB computes
A - X and nothing puts A back, so A becomes `0 - (n + (n-1) + ...)` and the
loop leaves when that RUNNING SUM is 0 mod 256 -- with `LDI A, 25` that is 49
passes and 147 clocks. **MEASURED: 49 loop bodies between two OUT TXPIN, and a
half-interval of 192 clocks on the wire where the listing said 120.** The
`LDI A, 0` moved INSIDE the loop, so the fourth instruction makes the branch a
comparison rather than an accumulation.

**AND THE SIXTH CHECK SIMULATES THOSE LOOPS INSTEAD OF CHARGING THEM A FLAT
RATE, because the version that assumed three clocks reported 120 for firmware
transmitting 60% slow.** A check that shares the assumption it exists to test
is a check that cannot fail, and this act has spent three sessions trying to
earn the right to say that sentence.

### FIVE MORE FAULTS IN THE ENCODER, ALL FOUND BY A PROBE PRINTING

1. **`frame_done` was NEVER REACHED and no structural check could see it.** The
   three-byte test compared 3 against a register loaded from `dmem[9]` BEFORE
   the increment, so on the third byte it computed 3 - 2. The jump is
   structurally perfect and the act's five checks all called it clean, while the
   three bytes arrived with nothing noticing they had all arrived. MEASURED by
   counting executions of the encoder's own `OUT`: **zero in a whole pass.**
   This is the fault the handoff's `<<wip>>` wiring was invented to prevent, in
   the place nobody was looking: not a red test but a green one over a branch
   that never fires.
2. **A masked value compared in the wrong domain.** The bit was extracted as
   `0x80`/`0x00` and tested against 1, which is zero for neither.
3. **A parity test that was not a parity test.** It asked whether the count was
   ZERO where it meant to ask whether it was ODD, so the mid route was dead --
   **0 entries in a pass** -- and a run of zeros reloaded its byte on every bit.
4. **The byte-spent test asked whether `dmem[11]` was `0x80`**, which is where
   the byte STARTS. It is 0 when the byte is spent, and a byte whose value is
   `0x00` is zero from the moment it is loaded, so the preamble reloaded 1330
   times in a pass for the five that belong there.
5. **THE ONE THAT WAS WORTH THE SESSION: the branch that keeps the level left A
   HOLDING THE TEST'S OWN RESULT.** `LDI A,0 / SUB A,X / JZ enc_keep_level`
   arrives with A = 0, and the five instructions after it were NOPs, which of
   course leave A alone -- so the pad was driven at `0x00` for every FIRST HALF
   of a bit whose value was a one. **MEASURED: 144 intervals of 121 clocks and
   not one of 241.** Invisible to every check here: the branch is taken, the
   label is right, the route is the length it was fitted to, and A is a value
   the block computed. What it needed was the level somewhere the branch could
   not reach, which is what Y is for.

**AND THE SIXTH FAULT IS ABOUT THE INSTRUMENT, NOT THE FIRMWARE.**
`bmc_checks.py`'s jump check read the encoded operand as `word & 0xFF`. The
target field is `arg[PCW-1:0]` and PCW is 10, so the check measured the wrong
field -- right only while the program is shorter than 256 words, which it
stopped being when the encoder landed. **It answered "13 mismatches" on a
firmware that assembles, links and runs.** A check that has only ever agreed
with a small program is a check that has never been asked a question.

### THE TESTBENCH'S DECODER, TO THE WIRE RULES

It folded two transitions per bit, which is the flaw this act was written to
catch. It now has the three-state phase lock, the preamble skip, and the flag
read off the preamble's levels as `0xFF` for FM0 and `0x00` for FM1. **It works
in MICROSECONDS derived from the clock count**, which is the receiver's own
timebase and the firmware's too: `I2CTICK` counts whole microseconds, so a
121-clock interval is 2 us to both of them. It now locks, and it banks three
bytes.

### WHAT IS RED, AND IT IS THE HONEST END STATE

The testbench's decoder recovers `80 00 80` for `a5 3c 96` and its flag is -1:
it has locked the clock and not the polarity. **Two properties of the wire are
unexplained and both are measured** -- the return leg carries 66 changes and
SIX two-half gaps per pass where the model says 63 and SIXTEEN, and the
preamble's assembled byte is neither `0xFF` nor `0x00`.

**NEXT, IN THIS ORDER, AND NONE OF IT IS A GUESS:**

1. `tb/probes/probe_tb_classify.v` -- the TB decoder's own classification
   trace. It shows the lock landing **two half-intervals early**: the first
   4 us gap emits a mid and so does the next one, which cannot happen inside
   the preamble's eight zeros. Either the firmware's preamble has a gap it
   should not have, or the TB is locking on the wrong transition, and the trace
   says which.
2. `tb/probes/probe_pad_intervals.v` -- where the three EXTRA changes per pass
   are. 66 changes against the model's 63 and 6 two-half gaps against 16 is
   three boundaries that transition where the rules say they do not.
3. Only then the encoder, and only if (1) says the wire is wrong.

**THE INSTRUMENTS ARE IN THE REPOSITORY** (`tb/probes/`, with `run.sh` and a
`labels.py` that emits `L_<label>` defines from the assembler's own listing, so
a probe can never again be pointed at a decimal). They are there because /tmp
